(i) implies only the bound on
\psi_{1/s}
-norm.

5
Functional estimation in exponential families

In this section, we consider an exponential family
\{P_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta\}
on a measurable space
(S,{\mathcal{A}})
equipped
with a measure
\mu.
The densities
p_{\theta}:=\frac{dP_{\theta}}{d\mu}
are of the form

\displaystyle p_{\theta}(x):=\frac{1}{Z(\theta)}\exp\{\langle T(x),\theta\rangle\},x\in S,

where statistic
T:S\mapsto E
takes values in a finite-dimensional Banach space
E
and parameter
\theta
takes values in the dual
space
E^{\ast}.
Since
E
is finite-dimensional, both
E
and
E^{\ast}
could be identified with
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
for some
N\geq 1.

However, in some examples (such as, for instance, exponential families with matrix parameter), it could be convenient to use different norms for the values
of
T
and for the parameter
\theta
(for instance, the operator norm and the nuclear norm). The normalizing constant

\displaystyle Z(\theta):=\int_{S}\exp\{\langle T(x),\theta\rangle\}\mu(dx),\theta\in E^{\ast}

is a convex function and the set
\Theta:=\{\theta\in E^{\ast}:Z(\theta)<+\infty\}
is convex.
This set is a natural parameter space for exponential family.

In what follows, we will assume that
\mu
is a probability measure. We also assume that
{\rm Int}\Theta\neq\emptyset,

that
{\rm l.s.}(\Theta)=E^{\ast}
and that, for all
u\in E^{\ast},c\in{\mathbb{R}},

\mu\{x:\langle T(x),u\rangle=c\}=0.

Under these assumptions, the exponential family is an identifiable statistical model. It is well known that more general
exponential families could be always reduced to this form by a proper change of measure
\mu
and by affine transformations of statistic
T(X)
and parameter
\theta
(see Section
8.1
for more details on this and other properties of exponential families).
Also, under the above assumptions the function
\psi(\theta):=\log Z(\theta)
is strictly convex in
\Theta
and its gradient

\displaystyle\Psi(\theta):=\psi^{\prime}(\theta)=(\nabla\psi)(\theta)={\mathbb{E}}_{\theta}T(X),\theta\in{\rm Int}\Theta

is a strictly monotone mapping, so, it is one-to-one. Moreover,
\Psi
is a
C^{\infty}
-diffeomorphism between
{\rm Int}\Theta

and its image. The last property makes it natural to re-parametrize the model and to use
t=\Psi(\theta)={\mathbb{E}}_{\theta}T(X)\in E

as a new parameter. It is usually called
the mean parameter
, but we will often use the terminology of Chencov
[
5
]
and call
it
the natural parameter
of exponential family.

It is also well known that

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\theta}=\Psi^{\prime}(\theta)={\rm Cov}_{\theta}(T(X))={\mathbb{E}}_{\theta}(T(X)-\Psi(\theta))\otimes(T(X)-\Psi(\theta))

is the covariance operator of
T(X),
acting from
E^{\ast}
to
E.
It is straightforward to check that
\Sigma_{\theta}
coincides
with the Fisher information
I(\theta)
of exponential family
\{P_{\theta}:\theta\in{\rm Int}\Theta\}.
On the other hand, the operator

{\mathcal{I}}(t)=\Sigma_{\Psi^{-1}(t)}^{-1},
acting from
E
into
E^{\ast},
coincides with the Fisher information of the model

\{P_{\Psi^{-1}(t)}:t\in\Psi({\rm Int}\Theta)\}
with natural parametrization.

Example 5.1
.

Suppose
\mu
is a Borel probability measure on
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
invariant with respect to the group of orthogonal transformations.
In this case,
\psi(\theta)=\varphi(\|\theta\|),
where
\varphi(\rho):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{N}}e^{\rho x_{1}}\mu(dx),\rho\geq 0.

Assume that
\varphi(\rho)<\infty
for some
\rho>0.
It is easy to see that
\Theta
is either the whole space
{\mathbb{R}}^{N},

or a ball of radius
r>0
centered at
0
(open or closed). Also, function
\varphi
is strictly convex and
\Phi(\rho):=\varphi^{\prime}(\rho)
is strictly
increasing. It is also easy to see that

\displaystyle\Psi(\theta)=\Phi(\|\theta\|)\frac{\theta}{\|\theta\|},\theta\in B(0,r)\ {\rm and}\ \Psi^{-1}(t)=\Phi^{-1}(\|t\|)\frac{t}{\|t\|},t\in B(0,\Phi(r)),

so, both
\Psi
and
\Psi^{-1}
are spherically symmetric and strictly monotone vector fields.
A special case of this example is so called von Mises-Fisher distribution, an important model for directional data.
In this case,
\mu
is a uniform distribution on
S^{N-1}.

For more details, see Example
8.1
.

We are interested in the problem of estimation of the value of a smooth functional
f(\theta)
of parameter
\theta
of exponential family
based on i.i.d. observations
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\sim P_{\theta}.
Our approach is based on reducing this problem to estimation of the value

f(\Psi^{-1}(t))
of smooth functional of the corresponding natural parameter
t=\Psi(\theta).
Since
\Psi
is a
C^{\infty}
-diffeomorphism,
the smoothness properties of functionals
f
and
f\circ\Psi^{-1}
are closely related (at least, at the points of the parameter space that are away
from its boundary). To estimate
(f\circ\Psi^{-1})(t),
we will use
\bar{T}_{n}:=\frac{T(X_{1})+\dots T(X_{n})}{n}
as a base estimator of

t={\mathbb{E}}_{\theta}T(X),
and construct estimator
T_{f\circ\Psi^{-1}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
and its truncated version

\tilde{T}_{f\circ\Psi^{-1}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
using the sample split, as described in Section
2
.

Example 5.2
.

Consider the problem of estimation of the entropy

\displaystyle H(\theta):=-\int_{S}p_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}d\mu

of unknown distribution from exponential family
P_{\theta},\theta\in\Theta

based on i.i.d. observations
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\sim P_{\theta}.

It is easy to check that

H(\theta)=-\psi^{\ast}({\mathbb{E}}_{\theta}T(X))=-\psi^{\ast}(\Psi(\theta)),

where

\displaystyle\psi^{\ast}(t):=\sup_{\theta\in E^{\ast}}[\langle t,\theta\rangle-\psi(\theta)],t\in E,

is the Legendre transform (or Young–Fenchel conjugate) of convex function
\psi(\theta)=\log Z(\theta).
Note also
that

\displaystyle\psi^{\ast}(t)=\langle t,\Psi^{-1}(t)\rangle-(\psi\circ\Psi^{-1})(t).

Thus, the problem of estimation of
H(\theta)
could be reduced to the problem of estimation of function
-\psi^{\ast}(t)
of natural
parameter
t.
For regular exponential models,
\psi^{\ast}
is an infinitely differentiable functional (see Section
8.1
for more detail), so, in principle, one can use estimator
\tilde{T}_{-\psi^{\ast}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n}),
based on the Taylor expansion of an arbitrary
order
m.
The quality of such estimators would depend on the Hölder
C^{s}
-norms of function
\psi^{\ast}
(as in the bounds of Theorem

5.1
below).

Suppose there exists a finite subset
{\mathcal{M}}
of
E^{\ast}
such that
\|u\|\leq 1,u\in{\mathcal{M}},

\log{\rm card}({\mathcal{M}})\leq d
for some parameter
d\geq 1

and, for some constant
C>0,

\displaystyle\|x\|\leq C\max_{u\in{\mathcal{M}}}|\langle x,u\rangle|.

(5.1)

Remark 5.1
.

Note that, if
E
is a Banach space of dimension
d,
then the dimension of
E^{\ast}
is also
d.
In this case, one can choose
set
{\mathcal{M}}
as a
1/2
-net for the unit ball of
E^{\ast}
of cardinality
\leq 5^{d}.
It is easy to see that condition (
5.1
)
holds with
C=2
and
\log({\rm card}({\mathcal{M}}))\leq(\log 5)d.

On the other hand, if
E
is the space of
d\times d
symmetric matrices equipped
with the operator norm, one can choose
{\mathcal{M}}:=\{u\otimes u:u\in{\mathcal{N}}\},
where
{\mathcal{N}}
is a
1/4
-net of the unit
ball in
\ell_{2}^{d}
of cardinality
\leq 9^{d}.
In this case, condition (
5.1
) still holds with
C=2
and

\log({\rm card}({\mathcal{M}}))\leq(2\log 9)d
(note that
{\rm dim}(E)=\frac{d(d+1}{2}
). This approach is useful in the following
example of exponential family with matrix parameter.

Example 5.3
.

Consider the following exponential model

\displaystyle P_{\theta}(dx)=\frac{1}{Z(\theta)}\exp\{\langle x\otimes x,\theta\rangle\}\mu(dx),x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},

where the parameter
\theta
is a symmetric
d\times d
matrix and
\mu
is a Borel probability measure in

{\mathbb{R}}^{d}.
The statistic generating this exponential family is
T(x)=x\otimes x.
It also takes values in the
space of symmetric matrices. This space will be equipped
with the operator norm and the resulting Banach space will be denotes by
E.
Its dual space
E^{\ast}
is again the linear space of
symmetric
d\times d
matrices equipped with the nuclear norm. With this conventions, statistic
T(x)=x\otimes x
takes values in
E

and the parameter space
\Theta=\{\theta:Z(\theta)<+\infty\}
is a convex subset of
E^{\ast}.
We assume that the functions

1,x_{i}x_{j}:1\leq i\leq j\leq d
are linearly independent on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and that

{\rm l.s.}(\Theta)=E^{\ast}.
Under these assumptions, the model is identifiable.
Note that

\Psi(\theta)={\mathbb{E}}_{\theta}(X\otimes X),X\sim P_{\theta},\theta\in\Theta,

which could be estimated by the sample covariance
\bar{T}_{n}:=n^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}X_{j}\otimes X_{j}.

In addition to mean zero normal model (with non-standard parametrization), this class of exponential families
includes Bingham distribution for directional data (with
\mu
being the uniform distribution on the unit sphere
S^{d-1}
)
and Ising model (with
\mu
being the uniform distribution on the binary cube
\{-1,1\}^{d}
).
For these two models, the support of measure
\mu
is bounded, which implies that
\Theta=E^{\ast}
(this, of course, could be the case even if the support of measure
\mu
is not bounded). See Example
8.2
for more detail.

Note that, for
\theta\in{\rm Int}\Theta,

\sigma_{\rm max}^{2}(\theta):=\|\Sigma_{\theta}\|
is the largest eigenvalue of the covariance
\Sigma_{\theta}
and
\sigma_{\rm min}^{2}(\theta):=\frac{1}{\|\Sigma_{\theta}^{-1}\|}
is its smallest eigenvalue.
Let
W\subset{\rm Int}\Theta
be an open set and let
G:=\Psi(W).

Denote

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm max}^{2}(W):=\sup_{\theta\in W}\sigma_{\rm max}^{2}(\theta),\ \ \sigma_{\rm min}^{2}(W):=\inf_{\theta\in W}\sigma_{\rm min}^{2}(\theta).

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
f:E^{\ast}\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}
be a uniformly bounded functional.
Suppose that, for some
m\geq 2,

f\circ\Psi^{-1}
is
m
times continuously differentiable in open set
G

and, moreover,

(f\circ\Psi^{-1})^{(m)}
satisfies the Hölder condition with exponent
\rho\in(0,1]
in
G.

Let numbers
\delta
and
r
be sufficiently large to satisfy the conditions

\displaystyle r\geq\frac{1}{\sigma_{\rm max}(W)}\sqrt{\frac{d}{n^{(0)}}}\ {\rm and}\ C^{\prime}C\sigma_{\rm max}(W)\sqrt{\frac{d}{n^{(0)}}}\leq\delta\leq 1

for a large enough numerical constant
C^{\prime}>0.
Let
U\subset G
and
V:=\psi^{-1}(U)\subset W
be such that, for all
t\in U,

B(t,\delta)\subset G

and, for all
\theta\in V,

B(\theta,r)\subset W.

(i) Assume that
n^{(0)}\asymp_{m}n
and
n_{j}^{(k)}\asymp_{m}n
,
j=1,\cdots,k
,
k=1,\cdots,m.

Then, for
M=\|f\|_{L_{\infty}},
estimator
\tilde{T}_{f\circ\Psi^{-1}}=\tilde{T}_{f\circ\Psi^{-1},M}
satisfies the bound

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in V}\left\|\tilde{T}_{f\circ\Psi^{-1}}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta)\right\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{\theta})}\lesssim_{s,C}\|f\circ\Psi^{-1}\|_{C^{s}(G)}\Bigl[\frac{\sigma_{\rm max}(W)}{\sqrt{n}}\bigvee\sigma_{\rm max}^{s}(W)\Bigl(\frac{d}{n}\Bigr)^{s/2}\Bigr].

(ii) Assume that, for all
j=1,\dots,k,k=1,\dots,m,

n_{j}^{(k)}\asymp_{m}n.

Then, for
M=\|f\|_{L_{\infty}}+\|f\|_{\rm Lip},
estimator
\tilde{T}_{f\circ\Psi^{-1}}=\tilde{T}_{f\circ\Psi^{-1},M}
satisfies the bound

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in V}\left\|\tilde{T}_{f\circ\Psi^{-1}}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta)-\langle\bar{T}_{1}^{(1)}-\Psi(\theta),(f\circ\Psi^{-1})^{\prime}(t)\rangle\right\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{\theta})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{s,C}\|f\circ\Psi^{-1}\|_{C^{s}(G)}\Bigl[\sigma_{\rm max}^{2}(W)\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\frac{d}{n^{(0)}}}\vee\sigma_{\rm max}^{s}(W)\Bigl(\frac{d}{n^{(0)}}\Bigr)^{s/2}\Bigr].

The bounds of Theorem
5.1
follow from more general bounds on the
L_{p}
-errors
of estimators
\tilde{T}_{f\circ\Psi^{-1}}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})
presented and proved
in Section
8
.

Corollary 5.1
.

Suppose the assumptions of Theorem
5.1
(i) hold.
Then, for
s>2,

\displaystyle\sup_{\|f\circ\Psi^{-1}\|_{C^{s}(G)}\leq 1}\sup_{\theta\in V}\|\tilde{T}_{f\circ\Psi^{-1}}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta)\|_{L_{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{\theta})}\lesssim_{s,C}\Bigl[\frac{\sigma_{\rm max}(W)}{\sqrt{n}}\bigvee\sigma_{\rm max}^{s}(W)\Bigl(\frac{d}{n}\Bigr)^{s/2}\Bigr]\wedge 1.

For
s\leq 2,
the same bound holds for the plug-in estimator
(f\circ\Psi^{-1})(\bar{T}_{n}).

The following result shows the minimax optimality of the upper bound of Corrolary
5.1
in the case of exponential families in the Euclidean space

E=E^{\ast}={\mathbb{R}}^{d},
provided that
\sigma_{\rm max}(W)\asymp\sigma_{\rm min}(W).

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
s>0,
let
\gamma>0
be a sufficiently small constant and suppose that open set
G
contains a ball
B_{\ell_{\infty}}(t_{0},\delta)

of radius
\delta\geq\gamma\sigma_{\rm min}(W)n^{-1/2}.

Then

\displaystyle\sup_{\|f\circ\Psi^{-1}\|_{C^{s}(G)}\leq 1}\inf_{\hat{T}_{n}}\sup_{\theta\in G}\|\hat{T}_{n}-f(\theta)\|_{L_{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{\theta})}\gtrsim\Bigl[\frac{\sigma_{\rm min}(W)}{\sqrt{n}}\bigvee\sigma_{\rm min}^{s}(W)\Bigl(\frac{d}{n}\Bigr)^{s/2}\Bigr]\wedge 1,

where the infimum is over all estimators
\hat{T}_{n}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
of functional
f(\theta).

The proof is given in Section
11
.

Denote by

\displaystyle\kappa(V):=\sup_{\theta\in V}\sup_{\|u\|\leq 1}\frac{\|\langle T(X)-{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta}T(X),u\rangle\|_{L_{4}({\mathbb{P}}_{\theta})}^{4}}{\|\langle T(X)-{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta}T(X),u\rangle\|_{L_{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{\theta})}^{4}}

the maximal kurtosis of r.v.
\langle T(X)-{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta}T(X),u\rangle,\|u\|\leq 1,\theta\in V.

It is not hard to check that

\displaystyle\kappa(V)\lesssim\sup_{\theta\in V}\sup_{u\neq 0}\frac{|\psi^{(4)}(\theta)[u,u,u,u]|}{\psi^{\prime\prime}(\theta)[u,u]^{2}}+1\lesssim\frac{\|\psi^{(4)}\|_{L_{\infty}(V)}}{\sigma_{\rm min}^{4}(V)}+1.

Corollary 5.2
.

Suppose the assumptions of Theorem
5.1
(ii) hold and, in addition,
n^{(0)}\asymp\frac{n}{\log n}.

Suppose that
\sigma_{\rm max}(W)\lesssim 1
and
\kappa(V)\lesssim 1.

Also suppose that
d\leq n^{\alpha}
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
s>\frac{1}{1-\alpha}.

Then

\displaystyle\sup_{\|f\circ\Psi^{-1}\|_{C^{s}(G)}\leq 1}\sup_{\theta\in V}W_{2,{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta}}\Bigl(\sqrt{n}(\tilde{T}_{f\circ\Psi^{-1}}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta)),\sigma_{f,\Psi}(\Psi(\theta))Z\Bigr)\to 0\ {\rm as}\ n\to\infty

(5.2)

and

\displaystyle\sup_{\|f\circ\Psi^{-1}\|_{C^{s}(G)}\leq 1}\sup_{\theta\in V}\Bigl|\sqrt{n}\Bigl\|\tilde{T}_{f\circ\Psi^{-1}}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta)\Bigr\|_{L_{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{\theta})}-\sigma_{f,\Psi}(\Psi(\theta))\Bigr|\to 0\ {\rm as}\ n\to\infty,

(5.3)

where

\displaystyle\sigma_{f,\Psi}^{2}(t):=\langle\Sigma_{\Psi^{-1}(t)}(f\circ\Psi^{-1})^{\prime}(t),(f\circ\Psi^{-1})^{\prime}(t)\rangle,t\in U.

The proof is given in Section
8.3
.

Remark 5.2
.

In
[
16
]
, a result similar to Corollary
5.2
was proved for functional estimators based on iterated
bootstrap, but only in the case of
log-conave
exponential families (that is, when measure
\mu
is log-concave). Its extension beyond
the log-concave case seems impossible, at least with the methods developed in
[
16
]
.

Remark 5.3
.

Note that

\displaystyle\sigma_{f,\Psi}^{2}(t)=\langle{\mathcal{I}}(t)^{-1}(f\circ\Psi^{-1})^{\prime}(t),(f\circ\Psi^{-1})^{\prime}(t)\rangle,

where
{\mathcal{I}}(t)
is the Fisher information for the model
\{P_{\Psi^{-1}(t)}:t\in G\}
with the natural parameter
t.

One can apply the local minimax bound of Theorem
2.3
to this model along with (
5.3
)
to show that estimator
\tilde{T}_{f\circ\Psi^{-1}}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})
is locally asymptotically minimax provided that
d\leq n^{\alpha}

for some
\alpha\in[1/2,1)
and
s>\frac{1}{1-\alpha}
(for
\alpha<1/2
and
s>\frac{1}{1-\alpha},
the plug-in estimator
(f\circ\Psi^{-1})(\bar{T}_{n})

is locally asymptotically minimax).

6
Upper bounds: further details and proofs

The proofs of the results of Section
2
easily follow from more general and somewhat technical facts stated and proved below.
For
k=1,\dots,m,
we assume that estimators
\hat{\theta}^{(0)},\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)},j=1,\dots,k
of parameter
\theta=\theta(P),
based on i.i.d. observations
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\sim P\in{\mathcal{P}},
are independent r.v.
We will use the following notations:

\displaystyle A_{p}(P,\delta):=\max_{1\leq k\leq m}\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\sup_{\|u\|\leq 1}\Bigl\|\langle\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta(P),u\rangle I(\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta(P)\|<\delta)\Bigr\|_{L_{p}({\mathbb{P}}_{P})}

and

\displaystyle B_{p}(P,\delta):=\max_{1\leq k\leq m}\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\Bigl\|\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta(P)\|I(\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta(P)\|<\delta)\Bigr\|_{L_{p}({\mathbb{P}}_{P})}.

We will also denote

\displaystyle\tilde{\beta}_{p}(P,\delta):=\Bigl\|\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta(P)\|I(\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta(P)\|<\delta)\Bigr\|_{L_{p}({\mathbb{P}}_{P})}.

For
\delta=+\infty,
we set
A_{p}(P):=A_{p}(P,+\infty),

B_{p}(P):=B_{p}(P,+\infty)
and
\tilde{\beta}_{p}(P):=\tilde{\beta}_{p}(P,+\infty)

Note that

\displaystyle A_{p}(P,\delta)\leq A_{p}(P)\wedge\delta,\ \ B_{p}(P,\delta)\leq B_{p}(P)\wedge\delta,\ \ \tilde{\beta}_{p}(P,\delta)\leq\tilde{\beta}_{p}(P)\wedge\delta.

Based on estimators
\hat{\theta}^{(0)},\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)},j=1,\dots,k,k=1,\dots,m,
we define estimator
T_{f}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
by (
2.3
)
and
estimator
\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})=\tilde{T}_{f,M}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
by (
2.4
).

The following result will be proved (which implies Theorem
2.1
).

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
s:=m+\rho
for some
m\geq 2
and
\rho\in(0,1],
and suppose that
f^{\prime}\in C^{s-1}(E).

Then

\displaystyle\left\|T_{f}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta(P))\right\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{P}_{P})}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{2^{k-2}\|f^{(k)}\|_{L_{\infty}}}{(k-1)!}A_{p}(P)\left(B_{p}^{k-1}(P)+\tilde{\beta}_{p(k-1)}^{k-1}(P)\right)+\frac{1}{m!}\|f^{(m)}\|_{{\rm Lip}_{\rho}}\tilde{\beta}_{ps}^{s}(P).

Moreover,

\displaystyle\left\|T_{f}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta(P))-\langle\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta(P),f^{\prime}(\theta(P))\rangle\right\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{P}_{P})}

\displaystyle\leq\|f^{\prime\prime}\|_{L_{\infty}}A_{p}(P)\tilde{\beta}_{p}(P)+\sum_{k=2}^{m}\frac{2^{k-2}\|f^{(k)}\|_{L_{\infty}}}{(k-1)!}A_{p}(P)\left(B_{p}^{k-1}(P)+\tilde{\beta}_{p(k-1)}^{k-1}(P)\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{m!}\|f^{(m)}\|_{{\rm Lip}_{\rho}}\tilde{\beta}_{ps}^{s}(P).

For truncated estimator
\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n}),
we will prove the following local version of the previous result, which implies Theorem
2.2
.

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
f:E\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}
be a uniformly bounded functional and let
\theta=\theta(P)
for some
P\in{\mathcal{P}}.

Suppose
f
is
m
times Frèchet continuously differentiable in
U:=B(\theta;\delta)
for some
\delta\in(0,1]
and, moreover,

f^{(m)}
satisfies the Hölder condition with exponent
\rho\in(0,1]
in
U.

Then, for
M\geq\|f\|_{L_{\infty}}
and for all
p^{\prime}\geq p,

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta(P))\right\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{P}_{P})}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{2^{k-2}\|f^{(k)}\|_{L_{\infty}(U)}}{(k-1)!}A_{p}(P,\delta)\left(B_{p}^{k-1}(P,\delta)+\tilde{\beta}_{p(k-1)}^{k-1}(P,\delta)\right)+\frac{1}{m!}\|f^{(m)}\|_{{\rm Lip}_{\rho}(U)}\tilde{\beta}_{ps}^{s}(P,\delta)

\displaystyle+(\|f\|_{L_{\infty}}+M)\biggl(\left(\frac{m(m+1)}{2}\right)^{1/p}\biggl(\frac{B_{p^{\prime}}(P)}{\delta}\biggr)^{p^{\prime}/p}+\biggl(\frac{\tilde{\beta}_{p^{\prime}}(P)}{\delta}\biggr)^{p^{\prime}/p}\biggr).

Moreover, if
M\geq\|f\|_{\infty}+\|f\|_{{\rm Lip}}\delta,
then, for all
p^{\prime}\geq p,

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta(P))-\langle\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta(P),f^{\prime}(\theta(P))\rangle\right\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{P}_{P})}

\displaystyle\leq\|f^{\prime\prime}\|_{L_{\infty}(U)}A_{p}(P,\delta)\tilde{\beta}_{p}(P,\delta)

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=2}^{m}\frac{2^{k-2}\|f^{(k)}\|_{L_{\infty}(U)}}{(k-1)!}A_{p}(P,\delta)\left(B_{p}^{k-1}(P,\delta)+\tilde{\beta}_{p(k-1)}^{k-1}(P,\delta)\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{m!}\|f^{(m)}\|_{{\rm Lip}_{\rho}(U)}\tilde{\beta}_{ps}^{s}(P,\delta)

\displaystyle+C_{m}(\|f\|_{L_{\infty}}+\|f^{\prime}\|_{L_{\infty}}+M)(1+B_{p}(P))\biggl(\biggl(\frac{B_{p^{\prime}}(P)}{\delta}\biggr)^{p^{\prime}/p}+\biggl(\frac{\tilde{\beta}_{p^{\prime}}(P)}{\delta}\biggr)^{p^{\prime}/p}\biggr),

where
C_{m}:=\frac{m(m-1)}{2}\vee\left(\frac{m(m+1)}{2}\right)^{1/p}+1.

We now turn to the proof of theorems
6.1
and
6.2
.

proof.

The proofs rely on the following simple lemmas.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
M[x_{1},\cdots,x_{k}]
be a bounded symmetric
k
-linear form on
E
and
\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{k}
be independent random variables in
E
. The following bound holds for all
p\geq 1
and
\delta>0
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left|M[\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{k}]I\left(\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\xi_{j}\|<\delta\right)\right|^{p}\leq\|M\|^{p}\sup_{\|u\|\leq 1}\mathbb{E}|\langle\xi_{k},u\rangle|^{p}I(\|\xi_{k}\|<\delta)\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}\mathbb{E}\|\xi_{j}\|^{p}I(\|\xi_{j}\|<\delta).

proof.

Define a
(k-1)
-linear form
\tilde{M}[x_{1},\dots,x_{k-1}]
on
E
with values in
E^{\ast}
as follows:

\displaystyle\langle x,\tilde{M}[x_{1},\dots,x_{k-1}]\rangle:=M[x_{1},\dots,x_{k-1},x],x\in E.

Clearly,

\displaystyle\|\tilde{M}\|

\displaystyle=\sup_{\|x_{1}\|,\dots,\|x_{k-1}\|\leq 1}\|\tilde{M}[x_{1},\dots,x_{k-1}]\|=\sup_{\|x_{1}\|,\dots,\|x_{k-1}\|\leq 1}\sup_{\|x\|\leq 1}\langle x,\tilde{M}[x_{1},\dots,x_{k-1}]\rangle

\displaystyle=\sup_{\|x_{1}\|,\dots,\|x_{k-1}\|,\|x\|\leq 1}M[x_{1},\dots,x_{k-1},x]=\|M\|.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left|M[\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{k}]I\left(\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\xi_{j}\|<\delta\right)\right|^{p}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\mathbb{E}_{\xi_{k}}\left|\left\langle\xi_{k},\tilde{M}[\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{k-1}]\right\rangle I\left(\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\xi_{j}\|<\delta\right)\right|^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\|u\|\leq 1}\mathbb{E}|\langle\xi_{k},u\rangle|^{p}I(\|\xi_{k}\|<\delta)\cdot\mathbb{E}\left\|\tilde{M}[\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{k-1}]\right\|^{p}I\left(\max_{1\leq j\leq k-1}\|\xi_{j}\|<\delta\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\|u\|\leq 1}\mathbb{E}|\langle\xi_{k},u\rangle|^{p}I(\|\xi_{k}\|<\delta)\cdot\|\tilde{M}\|^{p}\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}\|\xi_{j}\|^{p}\right)I\left(\max_{1\leq j\leq k-1}\|\xi_{j}\|<\delta\right)

\displaystyle\leq\|M\|^{p}\sup_{\|u\|\leq 1}\mathbb{E}|\langle\xi_{k},u\rangle|^{p}I(\|\xi_{k}\|<\delta)\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}\mathbb{E}\|\xi_{j}\|^{p}I\left(\|\xi_{j}\|<\delta\right),

implying the claim of the proposition.
∎

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
M[x_{1},\cdots,x_{k}]
be a bounded symmetric
k
-linear form on
E
. Let
\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{k}
be independent random variables in
E
and let
w\in E
. For
p\geq 1
, denote

\displaystyle A:=\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\sup_{\|u\|\leq 1}\|\langle\xi_{j}-w,u\rangle I(\|\xi_{j}-w\|<\delta)\|_{L_{p}}

and

\displaystyle B:=\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\Big\|\|\xi_{j}-w\|I(\|\xi_{j}-w\|<\delta)\Big\|_{L_{p}}.

The following bound holds for all
\delta>0
:

\displaystyle\left\|\left(M[\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{k}]-M[w,\cdots,w]\right)I\left(\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\xi_{j}-w\|<\delta\right)\right\|_{L_{p}}\leq k\|M\|A(B+\|w\|)^{k-1}.

proof.

We have

\displaystyle M[\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{k}]=\sum_{l=0}^{k}\sum_{0\leq j_{1}\dots<j_{l}\leq k}M[\xi_{j_{1}}-w,\dots,\xi_{j_{k}}-w,w,\underset{k-l}{\dots},w].

Denote

\displaystyle M_{w,l}[x_{1},\dots,x_{l}]:=M[x_{1},\dots,x_{l},w,\underset{k-l}{\dots},w].

Then

\displaystyle M[\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{k}]-M[w,\dots,w]=\sum_{l=1}^{k}\sum_{1\leq j_{1}\dots<j_{l}\leq k}M_{w,l}[\xi_{j_{1}}-w,\dots,\xi_{j_{k}}-w]

and also, for an
l
-linear form
M_{w,l},
we have

\displaystyle\|M_{w,l}\|\leq\|M\|\|w\|^{k-l}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\Big|M[\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{k}]-M[w,\cdots,w]\Big|I\left(\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\xi_{j}-w\|<\delta\right)

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{l=1}^{k}\sum_{1\leq j_{1}<\cdots<j_{l}\leq k}M_{w,l}[\xi_{j_{1}}-w,\cdots,\xi_{j_{l}}-w]\right|I\left(\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\xi_{j}-w\|<\delta\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l=1}^{k}\sum_{1\leq j_{1}<\cdots<j_{l}\leq k}\Big|M_{w,l}[\xi_{j_{1}}-w,\cdots,\xi_{j_{l}}-w]\Big|I\left(\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\xi_{j}-w\|<\delta\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l=1}^{k}\sum_{1\leq j_{1}<\cdots<j_{l}\leq k}\Big|M_{w,l}[\xi_{j_{1}}-w,\cdots,\xi_{j_{l}}-w]\Big|I\left(\max_{1\leq s\leq l}\|\xi_{j_{s}}-w\|<\delta\right).

Using the bound of Proposition
6.1
, we get

\displaystyle\left\|M_{w,l}[\xi_{j_{1}}-w,\cdots,\xi_{j_{l}}-w]I\left(\max_{1\leq s\leq l}\|\xi_{j_{s}}-w\|<\delta\right)\right\|_{L_{p}}\leq\|M\|\|w\|^{k-l}B^{l-1}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\left\|\left(M[\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{k}]-M[w,\cdots,w]\right)I\left(\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\xi_{j}-w\|<\delta\right)\right\|_{L_{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\|M\|A\sum_{l=1}^{k}\binom{k}{l}B^{l-1}\|w\|^{k-l}=\|M\|\frac{A}{B}\sum_{l=1}^{k}\binom{k}{l}B^{l}\|w\|^{k-l}

\displaystyle=\|M\|\frac{A}{B}((B+\|w\|)^{k}-\|w\|^{k})\leq\|M\|\frac{A}{B}\ k(B+\|w\|)^{k-1}B

\displaystyle\leq k\|M\|A(B+\|w\|)^{k-1}.

∎

Consider now the problem of estimation of the value of a smooth functional
f
for a value of the parameter
\theta=\theta(P)
in a neighborhood of a given point
t\in E.

Namely, we will assume that both
\theta
and
t
are in an open ball
U.

Suppose, for each
k=1,\dots,m,
we are in a possession of
independent
estimators
\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)},j=1,\dots,k
of parameter
\theta(P)
based on i.i.d. observations

X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\sim P\in{\mathcal{P}}.
Define

\displaystyle T_{f}(t;X_{1},\dots,X_{n}):=\sum_{k=0}^{m}\frac{f^{(k)}(t)[\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(k)}-t,\dots,\hat{\theta}_{k}^{(k)}-t]}{k!}.

Lemma 6.3
.

Under the above assumptions and notations, the following bound holds:

\displaystyle\left\|(T_{f}(t;X_{1},\dots,X_{n})-f(\theta(P)))I\left(\max_{1\leq k\leq m}\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta(P)\|<\delta\right)\right\|_{L_{p}({\mathbb{P}}_{P})}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{\|f^{(k)}(t)\|}{(k-1)!}A_{p}(P,\delta)(B_{p}(P,\delta)+\|t-\theta\|)^{k-1}+\frac{1}{m!}\|f^{(m)}\|_{{\rm Lip}_{\rho}(U)}\|t-\theta(P)\|^{s}.

(6.1)

Moreover,

\displaystyle\left\|(T_{f}(t;X_{1},\dots,X_{n})-f(\theta(P))-\langle\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta(P),f^{\prime}(\theta(P))\rangle)I\left(\max_{1\leq k\leq m}\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta(P)\|<\delta\right)\right\|_{L_{p}({\mathbb{P}}_{P})}

\displaystyle\leq\|f^{\prime\prime}\|_{L_{\infty}(U)}A_{p}(P,\delta)\|t-\theta(P)\|+\sum_{k=2}^{m}\frac{\|f^{(k)}(t)\|}{(k-1)!}A_{p}(P,\delta)(B_{p}(P,\delta)+\|t-\theta(P)\|)^{k-1}

(6.2)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{m!}\|f^{(m)}\|_{{\rm Lip}_{\rho}(U)}\|t-\theta(P)\|^{s}.

proof.

Note that, for
\theta=\theta(P)
by the Taylor expansion,

\displaystyle f(\theta)=\sum_{k=0}^{m}\frac{f^{(k)}(t)[\theta-t,\dots,\theta-t]}{k!}+R_{m},

with the remainder
R_{m}
satisfying the bound:

\displaystyle|R_{m}|\leq\frac{1}{m!}\|f^{(m)}\|_{{\rm Lip}_{\rho}(U)}\|\theta-t\|^{s},s=m+\rho.

(6.3)

For estimator
T_{f}(t;X_{1},\dots,X_{n}),
we have

\displaystyle T_{f}(t;X_{1},\dots,X_{n})-f(\theta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\Bigl(\frac{f^{(k)}(t)[\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(k)}-t,\dots,\hat{\theta}_{k}^{(k)}-t]}{k!}-\frac{f^{(k)}(t)[\theta-t,\dots,\theta-t]}{k!}\Bigr)-R_{m},

implying that, for
L_{p}=L_{p}({\mathbb{P}}_{P}),

\displaystyle\left\|(T_{f}(t;X_{1},\dots,X_{n})-f(\theta))\left(\max_{1\leq k\leq m}\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta\|<\delta\right)\right\|_{L_{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{1}{k!}\left\|f^{(k)}(t)[\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(k)}-t,\dots,\hat{\theta}_{k}^{(k)}-t]-f^{(k)}(t)[\theta-t,\dots,\theta-t]I\left(\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta\|<\delta\right)\right\|_{L_{p}}

(6.4)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ +|R_{m}|.

We will apply the bound of Lemma
6.2
to the
k
-linear form
f^{(k)}(t)
and to r.v.
\xi_{j}:=\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-t,j=1,\dots,k

with
w:=\theta-t.
We then have

\displaystyle A:=A_{k}

\displaystyle:=\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\sup_{\|u\|\leq 1}\|\langle\xi_{j}-w,u\rangle I(\|\xi_{j}-w\|<\delta)\|_{L_{p}}

\displaystyle=\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\sup_{\|u\|\leq 1}\|\langle\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta,u\rangle I(\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta\|<\delta)\|_{L_{p}}

and

\displaystyle B:=B_{k}

\displaystyle:=\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\|\xi_{j}-w\|I(\|\xi_{j}-w\|<\delta)\|_{L_{p}}

\displaystyle=\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta\|I(\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta\|<\delta)\|_{L_{p}},

and the bound of Lemma
6.2
yields

\displaystyle\left\|\left(f^{(k)}(t)[\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(k)}-t,\cdots,\hat{\theta}_{k}^{(k)}-t]-f^{(k)}(t)[\theta-t,\cdots,\theta-t]\right)I\left(\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta\|<\delta\right)\right\|_{L_{p}}

\displaystyle\leq k\|f^{(k)}(t)\|A_{k}(B_{k}+\|t-\theta\|)^{k-1}.

Substituting the last bound in (
6
) and also using (
6.3
), we get bound (
6.3
).

To prove the second bound, note that

\displaystyle T_{f}(t;X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta)-\langle\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta,f^{\prime}(\theta)\rangle=\langle\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta,f^{\prime}(t)-f^{\prime}(\theta)\rangle

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=2}^{m}\Bigl(\frac{f^{(k)}(t)[\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(k)}-t,\dots,\hat{\theta}_{k}^{(k)}-t]}{k!}-\frac{f^{(k)}(t)[\theta-t,\dots,\theta-t]}{k!}\Bigr)-R_{m}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\left\|(T_{f}(t;X_{1},\dots,X_{n})-f(\theta)-\langle\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta,f^{\prime}(\theta)\rangle)I\left(\max_{1\leq k\leq m}\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta\|<\delta\right)\right\|_{L_{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\langle\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta,f^{\prime}(t)-f^{\prime}(\theta)\rangle I\left(\|\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta\|<\delta\right)\right\|_{L_{p}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=2}^{m}\frac{\|f^{(k)}(t)\|}{(k-1)!}A_{p}(P,\delta)(B_{p}(P,\delta)+\|t-\theta\|)^{k-1}+\frac{1}{m!}\|f^{(m)}\|_{{\rm Lip}_{\rho}(U)}\|t-\theta\|^{s}

\displaystyle\leq\|f^{\prime}(t)-f^{\prime}(\theta)\|\sup_{\|u\|\leq 1}\left\|\langle\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta,u\rangle I\left(\|\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta\|<\delta\right)\right\|_{L_{p}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=2}^{m}\frac{\|f^{(k)}(t)\|}{(k-1)!}A_{p}(P,\delta)(B_{p}(P,\delta)+\|t-\theta\|)^{k-1}+\frac{1}{m!}\|f^{(m)}\|_{{\rm Lip}_{\rho}(U)}\|t-\theta\|^{s}

\displaystyle\leq\|f^{\prime\prime}\|_{L_{\infty}(U)}A_{p}(P,\delta)\|t-\theta\|+\sum_{k=2}^{m}\frac{\|f^{(k)}(t)\|}{(k-1)!}A_{p}(P,\delta)(B_{p}(P,\delta)+\|t-\theta\|)^{k-1}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{m!}\|f^{(m)}\|_{{\rm Lip}_{\rho}(U)}\|t-\theta\|^{s}.

∎

Since
T_{f}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})=T_{f}(\hat{\theta}^{(0)};X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
and, for all
k=1,\dots,m,
estimators
\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)},j=1,\dots,k
and
\hat{\theta}^{(0)}

are independent, we can use bound (
6.3
) conditionally on
\hat{\theta}^{(0)}
to get that, for
\theta=\theta(P)
on the event
\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|<\delta
(or,
\hat{\theta}^{(0)}\in U
)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{1/p}\left(\left|T_{f}(\hat{\theta}^{(0)};X_{1},\dots,X_{n})-f(\theta)\right|^{p}I\left(\max_{1\leq k\leq m}\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta\|<\delta\right)\Bigl|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{\|f^{(k)}(\hat{\theta}^{(0)})\|}{(k-1)!}A_{p}(P,\delta)(B_{p}(P,\delta)+\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|)^{k-1}+\frac{1}{m!}\|f^{(m)}\|_{{\rm Lip}_{\rho}(U)}\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|^{s}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{\|f^{(k)}\|_{L_{\infty}(U)}}{(k-1)!}A_{p}(P,\delta)(B_{p}(P,\delta)+\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|)^{k-1}+\frac{1}{m!}\|f^{(m)}\|_{{\rm Lip}_{\rho}(U)}\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|^{s}.

This yields

\displaystyle\left\|(T_{f}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta))I\left(\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|<\delta,\max_{1\leq k\leq m}\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta\|<\delta\right)\right\|_{L_{p}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\mathbb{E}^{1/p}\left(|T_{f}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta)|^{p}I\left(\max_{1\leq k\leq m}\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta\|<\delta\right)\Big|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}\right)\right\|_{L_{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{\|f^{(k)}\|_{L_{\infty}(U)}}{(k-1)!}A_{p}(P,\delta)\left\|(B_{p}(P,\delta)+\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|)^{k-1}I(\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|<\delta)\right\|_{L_{p}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{m!}\|f^{(m)}\|_{{\rm Lip}_{\rho}(U)}\left\|\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|^{s}I(\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|<\delta)\right\|_{L_{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{2^{k-1}\|f^{(k)}\|_{L_{\infty}(U)}}{(k-1)!}A_{p}(P,\delta)\left\|\left(\frac{B_{p}(P,\delta)+\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|}{2}\right)^{k-1}I(\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|<\delta)\right\|_{L_{p}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{m!}\|f^{(m)}\|_{{\rm Lip}_{\rho}(U)}\left\|\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|I(\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|<\delta)\right\|_{L_{ps}}^{s}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{2^{k-2}\|f^{(k)}\|_{L_{\infty}(U)}}{(k-1)!}A_{p}(P,\delta)\left(B_{p}^{k-1}(P,\delta)+\left\|\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|I(\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|<\delta)\right\|_{L_{p(k-1)}}^{k-1}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{m!}\|f^{(m)}\|_{{\rm Lip}_{\rho}(U)}\left\|\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|I(\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|<\delta)\right\|_{L_{ps}}^{s}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{2^{k-2}\|f^{(k)}\|_{L_{\infty}(U)}}{(k-1)!}A_{p}(P,\delta)\left(B_{p}^{k-1}(P,\delta)+\tilde{\beta}_{p(k-1)}^{k-1}(P,\delta)\right)+\frac{1}{m!}\|f^{(m)}\|_{{\rm Lip}_{\rho}(U)}\tilde{\beta}_{ps}^{s}(P,\delta).

(6.5)

Similarly, we can provide bounds on

\displaystyle\left\|(T_{f}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta)-\langle\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta,f^{\prime}(\theta)\rangle)I\left(\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|<\delta,\max_{1\leq k\leq m}\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta\|<\delta\right)\right\|_{L_{p}}.

To this end, we apply bound (
6.3
) to estimator
T_{f}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})=T_{f}(\hat{\theta}^{(0)};X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})
conditionally
on
\hat{\theta}^{(0)}.
Similarly to (
6
), we then have

\displaystyle\left\|(T_{f}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta)-\langle\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta,f^{\prime}(\theta)\rangle)I\left(\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|<\delta,\max_{1\leq k\leq m}\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta\|<\delta\right)\right\|_{L_{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\|f^{\prime\prime}\|_{L_{\infty}(U)}A_{p}(P,\delta)\tilde{\beta}_{p}(P,\delta)+\sum_{k=2}^{m}\frac{2^{k-2}\|f^{(k)}\|_{L_{\infty}(U)}}{(k-1)!}A_{p}(P,\delta)\left(B_{p}^{k-1}(P,\delta)+\tilde{\beta}_{p(k-1)}^{k-1}(P,\delta)\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{m!}\|f^{(m)}\|_{{\rm Lip}_{\rho}(U)}\tilde{\beta}_{ps}^{s}(P,\delta).

(6.6)

Setting
\delta=+\infty
in (
6
) and in (
6
) immediately yields the bound of Theorem
6.1
.

To complete the proof of Theorem
6.2
, note that, by the definition of truncated estimator
\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})

for
M\geq\|f\|_{\infty},

\displaystyle|\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})-f(\theta)|\leq|T_{f}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})-f(\theta)|

since
\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
is either equal to
T_{f}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n}),
or it is between
T_{f}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
and
f(\theta).

Thus, bound (
6
) implies that

\displaystyle\left\|(\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta))I\left(\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|<\delta,\max_{1\leq k\leq m}\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta\|<\delta\right)\right\|_{L_{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{2^{k-2}\|f^{(k)}\|_{L_{\infty}(U)}}{(k-1)!}A_{p}(P,\delta)\left(B_{p}^{k-1}(P,\delta)+\tilde{\beta}_{p(k-1)}^{k-1}(P,\delta)\right)+\frac{1}{m!}\|f^{(m)}\|_{{\rm Lip}_{\rho}(U)}\tilde{\beta}_{ps}^{s}(P,\delta).

(6.7)

On the other hand, since
|\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta)|\leq\|f\|_{L_{\infty}}+M,
we have

\displaystyle\left\|(\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta))I\left(\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|\geq\delta\ {\rm or}\ \max_{1\leq k\leq m}\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta\|\geq\delta\right)\right\|_{L_{p}}

\displaystyle\leq(\|f\|_{L_{\infty}}+M){\mathbb{P}}^{1/p}\biggl\{\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|\geq\delta\ {\rm or}\ \max_{1\leq k\leq m}\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta\|\geq\delta\biggr\}

\displaystyle\leq(\|f\|_{L_{\infty}}+M)\biggl({\mathbb{P}}\{\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|\geq\delta\}+\sum_{k=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\mathbb{P}}\{\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta\|\geq\delta\}\biggr)^{1/p}.

(6.8)

Note that, by Markov inequality,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta}\{\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|\geq\delta\}\leq\frac{{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta}\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|^{p^{\prime}}}{\delta^{p^{\prime}}}=\biggl(\frac{\tilde{\beta}_{p^{\prime}}(P)}{\delta}\biggr)^{p^{\prime}}

and, similarly,

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq k\leq m}\max_{1\leq j\leq k}{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta}\{\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta\|\geq\delta\}\leq\biggl(\frac{B_{p^{\prime}}(P)}{\delta}\biggr)^{p^{\prime}}.

This yields the bound

\displaystyle\left\|(\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta))I\left(\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|\geq\delta\ {\rm or}\ \max_{1\leq k\leq m}\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta\|\geq\delta\right)\right\|_{L_{p}}

\displaystyle\leq(\|f\|_{L_{\infty}}+M)\biggl(\left(\frac{m(m+1)}{2}\right)^{1/p}\biggl(\frac{B_{p^{\prime}}(P)}{\delta}\biggr)^{p^{\prime}/p}+\biggl(\frac{\tilde{\beta}_{p^{\prime}}(P)}{\delta}\biggr)^{p^{\prime}/p}\biggr),

which, together with (
6
), implies the first bound of Theorem
6.2
.

To prove the second bound, observe that, under the assumption
M\geq\|f\|_{L_{\infty}}+\|f\|_{\rm Lip}\delta,

the definition of estimator
\tilde{T}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
implies that

\displaystyle|\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})-f(\theta)-\langle\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta,f^{\prime}(\theta)\rangle|\leq|T(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})-f(\theta)-\langle\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta,f^{\prime}(\theta)\rangle|.

This follows from the fact that
|f(\theta)+\langle\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta,f^{\prime}(\theta)\rangle|\leq M
and, hence,
either
\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})=T_{f}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n}),

or
\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
is between
T_{f}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})

and
f(\theta)+\langle\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta,f^{\prime}(\theta)\rangle.
Therefore, it follows from (
6
) that

\displaystyle\left\|(\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta)-\langle\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta,f^{\prime}(\theta)\rangle)I\left(\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|<\delta,\max_{1\leq k\leq m}\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta\|<\delta\right)\right\|_{L_{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\|f^{\prime\prime}\|_{L_{\infty}(U)}A_{p}(P,\delta)\tilde{\beta}_{p}(P,\delta)+\sum_{k=2}^{m}\frac{2^{k-2}\|f^{(k)}\|_{L_{\infty}(U)}}{(k-1)!}A_{p}(P,\delta)\left(B_{p}^{k-1}(P,\delta)+\tilde{\beta}_{p(k-1)}^{k-1}(P,\delta)\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{m!}\|f^{(m)}\|_{{\rm Lip}_{\rho}(U)}\tilde{\beta}_{ps}^{s}(P,\delta).

(6.9)

On the other hand, bound (
6
) could be rewritten as follows:

\displaystyle\left\|(\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta)-\langle\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta,f^{\prime}(\theta)\rangle)I\left(\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|\geq\delta\ {\rm or}\ \max_{1\leq k\leq m}\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta\|\geq\delta\right)\right\|_{L_{p}}

\displaystyle\leq(\|f\|_{L_{\infty}}+M)\biggl({\mathbb{P}}\{\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|\geq\delta\}+\sum_{k=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\mathbb{P}}\{\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta\|\geq\delta\}\biggr)^{1/p}

\displaystyle+\|f^{\prime}(\theta)\|\left\|\|\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta\|I(\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|\geq\delta)\right\|_{L_{p}}+\|f^{\prime}(\theta)\|\left\|\|\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta\|I(\|\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta\|\geq\delta)\right\|_{L_{p}}

\displaystyle+\|f^{\prime}(\theta)\|\sum_{k=2}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\left\|\|\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta\|I(\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta\|\geq\delta)\right\|_{L_{p}}.

(6.10)

Note that

\displaystyle\left\|\|\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta\|I(\|\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta\|\geq\delta)\right\|_{L_{p}}\leq{\mathbb{E}}^{1/p}\left(\|\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta\|^{p}\frac{\|\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta\|^{p^{\prime}-p}}{\delta^{p^{\prime}-p}}\right)\leq\frac{B_{p^{\prime}}^{p^{\prime}/p}(P)}{\delta^{p^{\prime}/p-1}}.

In addition, using independence of estimators
\hat{\theta}^{(0)}
and
\hat{\theta}_{1}^{1},
we get

\displaystyle\left\|\|\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta\|I(\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|\geq\delta)\right\|_{L_{p}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\|\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta\|\right\|_{L_{p}}{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta}^{1/p}\{\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|\geq\delta\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{B_{p}(P)\tilde{\beta}_{p^{\prime}}^{p^{\prime}/p}(P)}{\delta^{p^{\prime}/p}}.

Similarly, for all
k=2,\dots,m,j=1,\dots,k,

\displaystyle\left\|\|\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta\|I(\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta\|\geq\delta)\right\|_{L_{p}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\|\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta\|\right\|_{L_{p}}{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta}^{1/p}\{\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta\|\geq\delta\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{B_{p}(P)B_{p^{\prime}}^{p^{\prime}/p}(P)}{\delta^{p^{\prime}/p}}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\left\|(\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta)-\langle\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta,f^{\prime}(\theta)\rangle)I\left(\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|\geq\delta\ {\rm or}\ \max_{1\leq k\leq m}\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta\|\geq\delta\right)\right\|_{L_{p}}

\displaystyle\leq(\|f\|_{L_{\infty}}+M)\biggl(\left(\frac{m(m+1)}{2}\right)^{1/p}\biggl(\frac{B_{p^{\prime}}(P)}{\delta}\biggr)^{p^{\prime}/p}+\biggl(\frac{\tilde{\beta}_{p^{\prime}}(P)}{\delta}\biggr)^{p^{\prime}/p}\biggr)

\displaystyle+\|f^{\prime}\|_{L_{\infty}}\biggl(\frac{B_{p^{\prime}}^{p^{\prime}/p}(P)}{\delta^{p^{\prime}/p-1}}+B_{p}(P)\biggl(\frac{\tilde{\beta}_{p^{\prime}}(P)}{\delta}\biggr)^{p^{\prime}/p}+\frac{m(m-1)}{2}B_{p}(P)\biggl(\frac{B_{p^{\prime}}(P)}{\delta}\biggr)^{p^{\prime}/p}\biggr).

Note also that

\displaystyle\|f^{\prime}\|_{L_{\infty}}\frac{B_{p^{\prime}}^{p^{\prime}/p}(P)}{\delta^{p^{\prime}/p-1}}=\|f^{\prime}\|_{L_{\infty}}\delta\biggl(\frac{B_{p^{\prime}}(P)}{\delta}\biggr)^{p^{\prime}/p}\leq M\biggl(\frac{B_{p^{\prime}}(P)}{\delta}\biggr)^{p^{\prime}/p},

which implies

\displaystyle\left\|(\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta)-\langle\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta,f^{\prime}(\theta)\rangle)I\left(\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|\geq\delta\ {\rm or}\ \max_{1\leq k\leq m}\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta\|\geq\delta\right)\right\|_{L_{p}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{m}(\|f\|_{L_{\infty}}+\|f^{\prime}\|_{L_{\infty}}+M)(1+B_{p}(P))\biggl(\biggl(\frac{B_{p^{\prime}}(P)}{\delta}\biggr)^{p^{\prime}/p}+\biggl(\frac{\tilde{\beta}_{p^{\prime}}(P)}{\delta}\biggr)^{p^{\prime}/p}\biggr).

where
C_{m}:=\frac{m(m-1)}{2}\vee\left(\frac{m(m+1)}{2}\right)^{1/p}+1.

Together with (
6
), this completes the proof of the second bound.

∎

7
Remarks on Bernstein type bounds in functional estimation

For the sample mean
\bar{X}_{n}
of i.i.d. observations
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
of real valued r.v.
X
uniformly bounded by constant
U>0

and with variance
\sigma,
the Bernstein inequality could be written
in the following form: for all
t\geq 0
with probability at least
1-e^{-t}

\displaystyle|\bar{X}_{n}-{\mathbb{E}}X|\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{\frac{t}{n}}\vee U\frac{t}{n}.

It is equivalent to the following bounds on the
L_{p}
-norms: for all
p\geq 1,

\displaystyle\|\bar{X}_{n}-{\mathbb{E}}X\|_{L_{p}}\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\vee U\frac{p}{n}.

Moreover, if
X
is a subexponential r.v. (not necessarily bounded) with
\|X\|_{\psi_{1}}\asymp\sigma,

then similar bounds hold with
U=\sigma.
If
X
is a subgaussian r.v. with
\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}\asymp\sigma,
then we
have that, for all
t\geq 0
with probability at least
1-e^{-t},

|\bar{X}_{n}-{\mathbb{E}}X|\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{\frac{t}{n}}

and, for all
p\geq 1,

\|\bar{X}_{n}-{\mathbb{E}}X\|_{L_{p}}\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}.

More generally, for a number of important estimators
\hat{\theta}_{n}
of parameter
\theta(P),
similar bounds on the
L_{p}
norms of

\langle\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta(P),u\rangle,u\in E^{\ast}

and
\|\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta(P)\|
hold for all
p\geq 1

and imply Bernstein type inequalities.

The properties of base estimators
\hat{\theta}_{n}
are postulated in the following assumptions.

Assumption 7.1
.

There exist functions
\sigma:{\mathcal{P}}\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and
U:{\mathcal{P}}\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

such that, for all
p\geq 1,

\displaystyle\sup_{\|u\|\leq 1}\Bigl\|\langle\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta(P),u\rangle\Bigr\|_{L_{p}({\mathbb{P}}_{P})}\lesssim\sigma(P)\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\vee U(P)\frac{p}{n},P\in{\mathcal{P}}.

In this case, it will be said that
\hat{\theta}_{n}
is a
Bernstein type estimator
.
If
U(P)=0,P\in{\mathcal{P}}
the above bound simplifies as follows:

\displaystyle\sup_{\|u\|\leq 1}\Bigl\|\langle\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta(P),u\rangle\Bigr\|_{L_{p}({\mathbb{P}}_{P})}\lesssim\ \sigma(P)\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}},P\in{\mathcal{P}},

and it will be said that
\hat{\theta}_{n}
is a
subgaussian type estimator
.

Assumption 7.2
.

There exist functions
\sigma:{\mathcal{P}}\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}_{+},

U:{\mathcal{P}}\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}_{+},

d_{1}:{\mathcal{P}}\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and
d_{2}:{\mathcal{P}}\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

such that, for all
p\geq 1,

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\|\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta(P)\|\Bigr\|_{L_{p}({\mathbb{P}}_{P})}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{d_{1}(P)}{n}}\vee\frac{d_{2}(P)}{n}\vee\sigma(P)\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\vee U(P)\frac{p}{n},P\in{\mathcal{P}}.

As in Section
2
, we consider the problem of estimation of functionals
f(\theta(P))
based on i.i.d. observations
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\sim P,P\in{\mathcal{P}}.

We use base estimator
\hat{\theta}_{n}
of
\theta(P)
and the sample split to construct
estimator
T_{f}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
of
f(\theta(P))
defined by (
2.3
) and its truncated version
\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
defined by (
2.4
).
For base estimators
\hat{\theta}_{n}
satisfying Assumptions
7.1
and
7.2
,
the following result holds.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
f:E\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}
be a uniformly bounded functional and let
\theta=\theta(P)
for some
P\in{\mathcal{P}}.

Suppose
f
is
m
times Frèchet continuously differentiable in
U:=B(\theta;\delta)
for some
\delta\in(0,1]
and some
m\geq 2,
and, moreover,

f^{(m)}
satisfies the Hölder condition with exponent
\rho\in(0,1]
in
U.

Assume that for a sufficiently large constant
C>0,

\displaystyle C\left(\sqrt{\frac{d_{1}(P)}{n^{(0)}}}\vee\frac{d_{2}(P)}{n^{(0)}}\right)\leq\delta

and also that assumptions
7.1
and
7.2
hold.

(i) Suppose that
n^{(0)}\asymp_{m}n
and
n_{j}^{(k)}\asymp_{m}n
,
j=1,\cdots,k
,
k=1,\cdots,m.

Then, for
M\geq\|f\|_{L_{\infty}},

p\geq 1
and for a constant
c>0
depending on
m,C,

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta(P))\right\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{P}_{P})}\lesssim_{s,C}\max_{1\leq k\leq m}\|f^{(k)}\|_{L_{\infty}(U)}\left(\sigma(P)\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\vee U(P)\frac{p}{n}\right)

\displaystyle+\|f^{(m)}\|_{{\rm Lip}_{\rho}(U)}\left(\Bigl(\frac{d_{1}(P)}{n}\Bigr)^{s/2}\vee\Bigl(\frac{d_{2}(P)}{n}\Bigr)^{s}\vee\sigma^{s}(P)\Bigl(\frac{p}{n}\Bigr)^{s/2}\vee U^{s}(P)\Bigl(\frac{p}{n}\Bigr)^{s}\right)

\displaystyle+(\|f\|_{L_{\infty}}+M)\exp\Bigl\{-c\frac{n}{p}\Bigl(\frac{\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}(P)}\wedge\frac{\delta}{U(P)}\Bigr)\Bigr\}.

(ii) Suppose that, for all
j=1,\dots,k,k=1,\dots,m,

n_{j}^{(k)}\asymp_{m}n.

If
M\geq\|f\|_{\infty}+\|f\|_{{\rm Lip}}\delta,
then

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta(P))-\langle\hat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\theta,f^{\prime}(\theta(P))\rangle\right\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{P}_{P})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{s,C}\max_{2\leq k\leq m}\|f^{(k)}\|_{L_{\infty}(U)}\left(\sigma(P)\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\vee U(P)\frac{p}{n}\right)\times

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\sqrt{\frac{d_{1}(P)}{n^{(0)}}}\vee\frac{d_{2}(P)}{n^{(0)}}\vee\sigma(P)\sqrt{\frac{p}{n^{(0)}}}\vee U(P)\frac{p}{n^{(0)}}\right)

\displaystyle+\|f^{(m)}\|_{{\rm Lip}_{\rho}(U)}\left(\Bigl(\frac{d_{1}(P)}{n^{(0)}}\Bigr)^{s/2}\vee\Bigl(\frac{d_{2}(P)}{n^{(0)}}\Bigr)^{s}\vee\sigma^{s}(P)\Bigl(\frac{p}{n^{(0)}}\Bigr)^{s/2}\vee U^{s}(P)\Bigl(\frac{p}{n^{(0)}}\Bigr)^{s}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\|f\|_{L_{\infty}}+M+\|f^{\prime}(\theta)\|\left(\sqrt{\frac{d_{1}(P)}{n}}\vee\frac{d_{2}(P)}{n}\vee\sigma(P)\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\vee U(P)\frac{p}{n}\right)\right)\times

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \exp\Bigl\{-c\frac{n^{(0)}}{p}\Bigl(\frac{\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}(P)}\wedge\frac{\delta}{U(P)}\Bigr)\Bigr\}.

Since
e^{-x}\leq x^{-1},x>0,
the bound of Theorem
7.1
, (i) implies the following somewhat simplified inequality (for
M=\|f\|_{L_{\infty}}
):

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta(P))\right\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{P}_{P})}\lesssim_{s,C}\max_{0\leq k\leq m}\|f^{(k)}\|_{L_{\infty}(U)}\left(\sigma(P)\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\vee\frac{\sigma^{2}(P)}{\delta^{2}}\frac{p}{n}\vee\frac{U(P)}{\delta}\frac{p}{n}\right)

\displaystyle+\|f^{(m)}\|_{{\rm Lip}_{\rho}(U)}\left(\Bigl(\frac{d_{1}(P)}{n}\Bigr)^{s/2}\vee\Bigl(\frac{d_{2}(P)}{n}\Bigr)^{s}\right)+\|f^{(m)}\|_{{\rm Lip}_{\rho}(U)}\left(\sigma^{s}(P)\Bigl(\frac{p}{n}\Bigr)^{s/2}\vee U^{s}(P)\Bigl(\frac{p}{n}\Bigr)^{s}\right),

which also implies that

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta(P))\right\|_{L_{\psi_{1/s}}(\mathbb{P}_{P})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{s,C}\max_{0\leq k\leq m}\|f^{(k)}\|_{L_{\infty}(U)}\left(\frac{\sigma(P)}{\sqrt{n}}\vee\frac{\sigma^{2}(P)}{\delta^{2}n}\vee\frac{U(P)}{\delta n}\right)

\displaystyle+\|f^{(m)}\|_{{\rm Lip}_{\rho}(U)}\left(\Bigl(\frac{d_{1}(P)}{n}\Bigr)^{s/2}\vee\Bigl(\frac{d_{2}(P)}{n}\Bigr)^{s}\right).

Moreover, in the subgaussian case (when
U(P)=0
and
d_{2}(P)=0
), we have for all
p\geq 1

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta(P))\right\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{P}_{P})}\lesssim_{s,C}\max_{0\leq k\leq m}\|f^{(k)}\|_{L_{\infty}(U)}\left(\sigma(P)\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\vee\frac{\sigma^{2}(P)}{\delta^{2}}\frac{p}{n}\right)

\displaystyle+\|f^{(m)}\|_{{\rm Lip}_{\rho}(U)}\Bigl(\frac{d_{1}(P)}{n}\Bigr)^{s/2}+\|f^{(m)}\|_{{\rm Lip}_{\rho}(U)}\sigma^{s}(P)\Bigl(\frac{p}{n}\Bigr)^{s/2}

and

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(\theta(P))\right\|_{L_{\psi_{1/s}}(\mathbb{P}_{P})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{s,C}\max_{0\leq k\leq m}\|f^{(k)}\|_{L_{\infty}(U)}\left(\frac{\sigma(P)}{\sqrt{n}}\vee\frac{\sigma^{2}(P)}{\delta^{2}n}\right)+\|f^{(m)}\|_{{\rm Lip}_{\rho}(U)}\Bigl(\frac{d_{1}(P)}{n}\Bigr)^{s/2}.

To prove Theorem
7.1
, one just needs to modify the part of the argument in the proof of Theorem
6.2
related to bounding the probability of event

\displaystyle\biggl\{\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta\|\geq\delta\ {\rm or}\ \max_{1\leq k\leq m}\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta\|\geq\delta\biggr\},

where
\theta=\theta(P).

proof.

Note that, under Assumption
7.2
for a sufficiently large constant
C>0,
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{P}\left\{\|\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta(P)\|\geq C\left(\sqrt{\frac{d_{1}(P)}{n}}\vee\frac{d_{2}(P)}{n}\vee\sigma(P)\sqrt{\frac{t}{n}}\vee U(P)\frac{t}{n}\right)\right\}\leq e^{-t}.

Under the assumption that

\displaystyle C\left(\sqrt{\frac{d_{1}(P)}{n}}\vee\frac{d_{2}(P)}{n}\right)\leq\delta,

we can set

\displaystyle t:=n\left(\frac{1}{C^{2}}\frac{\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}(P)}\wedge\frac{1}{C}\frac{\delta}{U(P)}\right).

This yields the bound

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{P}\{\|\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta(P)\|\geq\delta\}\leq\exp\left\{-cn\left(\frac{\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}(P)}\wedge\frac{\delta}{U(P)}\right)\right\}

that holds for a sufficiently small constant
c>0.
Under the assumption
that
n^{(0)}\asymp_{m}n
and
n_{j}^{(k)}\asymp_{m}n
,
j=1,\cdots,k
,
k=1,\cdots,m,
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{P}^{1/p}\biggl\{\|\hat{\theta}^{(0)}-\theta(P)\|\geq\delta\ {\rm or}\ \max_{1\leq k\leq m}\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\hat{\theta}_{j}^{(k)}-\theta(P)\|\geq\delta\biggr\}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{m}\exp\left\{-c\frac{n}{p}\left(\frac{\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}(P)}\wedge\frac{\delta}{U(P)}\right)\right\}

for some
c>0.

Together with the rest of the argument of the proof of Theorem
6.2
, it is now easy to complete the proof of statement (i) of Theorem
7.1

and, similarly, conclude the proof of statement (ii).

∎

It is well known that Assumption
7.1
is equivalent to the following Bernstein type inequality: for all
P\in{\mathcal{P}},

for all
u\in E^{\ast}
with
\|u\|\leq 1
and for all
t\geq 1,

with probability
{\mathbb{P}}_{P}
at least
1-e^{-t}

\displaystyle|\langle\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta(P),u\rangle|\lesssim\sigma(P)\sqrt{\frac{t}{n}}\vee U(P)\frac{t}{n}.

(7.1)

To obtain Bernstein type bounds on
\|\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta(P)\|,
the following assumption on the norm of Banach space
E
is useful.

Assumption 7.3
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{M}}
is a finite set of linear functionals
u\in E^{\ast}
with
\|u\|\leq 1
such that

\displaystyle\|x\|\leq C\max_{u\in{\mathcal{M}}}|\langle x,u\rangle|,x\in E

(7.2)

with a numerical constant
C>0
and
\log{\rm card}({\mathcal{M}})\leq d
for some
d\geq 1.

Proposition 7.1
.

Under Assumptions
7.1
and
7.3
for all
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
and for all
p\geq 1,

\displaystyle\left\|\|\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta(P)\|\right\|_{L_{p}({\mathbb{P}}_{P})}\lesssim C\left[\sigma(P)\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}}\vee U(P)\frac{d}{n}\vee\sigma(P)\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\vee U(P)\frac{p}{n}\right].

In particular, under Assumption
7.1
with
U(P)=0,

\displaystyle\left\|\|\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta(P)\|\right\|_{L_{p}({\mathbb{P}}_{P})}\lesssim C\left[\sigma(P)\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}}\vee\sigma(P)\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\right].

proof.

It is enough to replace in bound (
7.1
)
t
by
t+\log M
for
M:={\rm card}({\mathcal{M}})
and apply this bound to all
u\in{\mathcal{M}}

and, finally, use the union bound to prove that, for all
t\geq 1,
with probability at least
1-e^{-t},

\displaystyle\|\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta(P)\|\lesssim C\sigma(P)\sqrt{\frac{\log M}{n}}\vee CU(P)\frac{\log M}{n}\vee C\sigma(P)\sqrt{\frac{t}{n}}\vee CU(P)\frac{t}{n}.

The claim now follows from a standard derivation of the bounds on the
L_{p}
-norms from the exponential inequality.

∎

Thus, under Assumptions
7.1
and
7.3
,
the bound of Assumption
7.2
holds with
d_{1}(P):=\sigma^{2}(P)d
and
d_{2}(P):=U(P)d.

Example 7.1
.

If
{\rm dim}(E)=d,
then, by a standard volumetric argument, there exists a
1/2
-net
{\mathcal{M}}
for the unit ball
\{u\in E^{\ast}:\|u\|\leq 1\}

of cardinality
M\leq 5^{d},
and we have

\displaystyle\|x\|\leq 2\max_{u\in{\mathcal{M}}}|\langle x,u\rangle|,x\in E.

This yields the bound

\displaystyle\left\|\|\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta(P)\|\right\|_{L_{p}({\mathbb{P}}_{P})}\lesssim\sigma(P)\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}}\vee U(P)\frac{d}{n}\vee\sigma(P)\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\vee U(P)\frac{p}{n}

(7.3)

that holds for all
p\geq 1.

Example 7.2
.

Let
E
be the space of linear operators
A:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
equipped with the operator norm.
Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be the set of operators
u\otimes v,u,v\in{\mathcal{N}},
where
{\mathcal{N}}
is a
1/4
-net for the unit ball of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
of cardinality

\leq 9^{d}.
Then,
M={\rm card}({\mathcal{M}})\leq 9^{2d}
and

\displaystyle\|A\|\leq 2\max_{B\in{\mathcal{M}}}|\langle A,B\rangle|=2\max_{u,v\in{\mathcal{N}}}|\langle Au,v\rangle|,A\in E.

Thus, we again get bound (
7.3
) for all
p\geq 1.

Note that, in this example,
{\rm dim}(E)=d^{2},
but the bound rather depends on the dimension
d
of space
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}.

As the above examples show, the quantity
\log{\rm card}({\mathcal{M}})
is often upper bounded by the dimension or other similar complexity parameters
d.

Bernstein type bounds on the norm
\|\hat{\theta}-\theta(P)\|
could be also often deduced from concentration inequalities for

\|\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta(P)\|
around its expectation and the bounds on
{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\|\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta(P)\|.

Example 7.3
.

Let
{\mathcal{P}}={\mathcal{P}}(S)
be the set of all probability
measures on a measurable space
(S,{\mathcal{A}})
and let the goal be to estimate the value of
f(P)
based
on i.i.d. observations
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\sim P
for a real valued functional
f:{\mathcal{P}}(S)\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}.

Denote by
W(S)
the space of all signed measures on
(S,{\mathcal{A}})
with a bounded total variation.
Clearly,
{\mathcal{P}}(S)\subset W(S).

Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be a class of
{\mathcal{A}}
-measurable functions
g:S\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}
such
that

\displaystyle|g(x)|\leq U,x\in S,g\in{\mathcal{G}}.

For all
\nu\in W(S),

\displaystyle\|\nu\|_{\mathcal{G}}:=\sup_{g\in{\mathcal{G}}}|\nu(g)|<+\infty,

where
\nu(g):=\nu g:=\int_{S}gd\nu.

In what follows, we equip the space
W(S)
with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{G}}
(instead of the usual total variation norm).
To emphasize the dependence of the norm on
{\mathcal{G}},
we will also call this space
W_{\mathcal{G}}.

We will view
(W_{\mathcal{G}},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{G}})
as a Banach space
E
and set
\theta(P):=P,P\in{\mathcal{P}}(S).

The empirical distribution
P_{n}:=n^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{k}},
where
\delta_{x}
denotes the unit point mass at
x\in S,

will be used as an estimator
\hat{\theta}_{n}
of
\theta(P)=P.

Let

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}=\sigma_{P}^{2}({\mathcal{G}}):=\sup_{g\in{\mathcal{G}}}(Pg^{2}-(Pg)^{2}).

We will also assume that there exists
V:=V({\mathcal{G}})\geq 1
such that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\|P_{n}-P\|_{\mathcal{G}}\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{\frac{V}{n}}\vee U\frac{V}{n}.

(7.4)

Conditions of this type often hold for empirical processes. For instance, if the uniform
L_{2}
-covering numbers of class

{\mathcal{G}}
satisfy the bound

\displaystyle\sup_{Q\in{\mathcal{P}}(S)}N({\mathcal{G}};L_{2}(Q),\varepsilon)\leq\Bigl(\frac{AU}{\varepsilon}\Bigr)^{v},\varepsilon>0,

for some
v>0,A>0,
then bound (
7.4
) holds with
V:=v\log\frac{AU}{\sigma}
(see
[
12
]
, p. 46).

Suppose that
{\mathcal{G}}
is equipped with some topology
\tau
such that it is a first countable compact Hausdorff space
and functions
{\mathcal{G}}\ni g\mapsto g(x)
are continuous for all
x\in S.

Let
f:{\mathcal{P}}(S)\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}
be a bounded Lipschitz functional with respect to the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{G}}.

By McShane-Whitney extension theorem,
f
can be extended to a bounded Lipschitz functional on the whole space

(W_{\mathcal{G}},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{G}})

with preservation of its sup-norm and its Lipschitz constant (such an extension will be still denoted by
f
). Let
P\in{\mathcal{P}}(S)
and let
V\subset W_{\mathcal{G}}=W(S)
be a neighborhood of
P.
We will assume that there exists a bounded Lipschitz extension of
f

such that
f\in C^{s}(V)
for a given
s>0
(some weaker notions of smoothness of
f
would be also sufficient for our purposes, but, for simplicity,
we will stick to the above definition). We are now in a position to apply the bounds of Theorem
7.1
to estimator

\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
of
f(P)
based on the empirical distribution
P_{n}.
For simplicity, we state the result only for
p=2.

Proposition 7.2
.

Let
\delta\in(0,1]
and let
V:=B(P,\delta)\subset W_{\mathcal{G}}.

Suppose
s:=m+\rho
for some
m\geq 2
and
\rho\in(0,1].

Assume that,
for a sufficiently large constant
C>0,

\displaystyle C\left(\sigma\sqrt{\frac{V}{n^{(0)}}}\vee U\frac{V}{n^{(0)}}\right)\leq\delta.

Then, for estimator
\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
with
M=\|f\|_{L_{\infty}},
the following bound holds:

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{T}_{f}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})-f(P)\right\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{P})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{s,C}\|f\|_{C^{s}(V)}\left[\left(\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}}\vee\frac{U}{n}\right)+\left(\sigma\sqrt{\frac{V}{n}}\vee U\frac{V}{n}\right)^{s}+\exp\Bigl\{-c_{1}n\Bigl(\frac{\delta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\wedge\frac{\delta}{U}\Bigr)\Bigr\}\right].

proof.

We start with the following lemma.

Lemma 7.1
.

For all
P\in{\mathcal{P}}(S)
and all
p\geq 1,

\displaystyle\sup_{u\in W_{\mathcal{G}}^{\ast},\|u\|\leq 1}\Bigl\|\langle P_{n}-P,u\rangle\Bigr\|_{L_{p}({\mathbb{P}}_{P})}\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\vee U\frac{p}{n}.

proof.

As a consequence of Bernstein inequality, for all
P\in{\mathcal{P}}(S),
all
p\geq 1
and all
g\in{\mathcal{G}},

\displaystyle\|(P_{n}-P)g\|_{L_{p}({\mathbb{P}}_{P})}\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\vee U\frac{p}{n}.

(7.5)

Uniform boundedness of
{\mathcal{G}}
and continuity of functions
{\mathcal{G}}\ni g\mapsto g(x),x\in S
implies
that
{\mathcal{G}}\ni g\mapsto\int_{S}gd\nu
is continuous in topology
\tau
for any signed measure
\nu\in W(S).

Thus,
W_{\mathcal{G}}
could be viewed as a linear subspace of the space of continuous functions
C({\mathcal{G}})

equipped with the sup-norm. By Hahn-Banach Theorem, any bounded linear functional
u\in W_{\mathcal{G}}^{\ast}

could be extended to a bounded linear functional on
C({\mathcal{G}})
with preservation of its norm. By Riesz-Markov Theorem, the dual space
of
C({\mathcal{G}})
can be identified with the space of Borel signed measures on

{\mathcal{G}}
equipped with the total variation norm. Thus, functional
u\in W_{\mathcal{G}}^{\ast}
can
be represented as an integral with respect to a Borel signed measure
\gamma
of total variation
\|\gamma\|=\|u\|
on
the space
{\mathcal{G}}.
Setting
\xi_{n}:=P_{n}-P,
we get

\displaystyle\sup_{u\in W_{\mathcal{G}}^{\ast},\|u\|\leq 1}\Bigl\|\langle P_{n}-P,u\rangle\Bigr\|_{L_{p}({\mathbb{P}}_{P})}\leq\sup_{\gamma\in C({\mathcal{G}})^{\ast},\|\gamma\|\leq 1}\Bigl\|\int_{{\mathcal{G}}}\xi_{n}(g)\gamma(dg)\Bigr\|_{L_{p}({\mathbb{P}}_{P})}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\gamma\in C({\mathcal{G}})^{\ast},\|\gamma\|\leq 1}{\mathbb{E}}^{1/p}\Bigl|\int_{{\mathcal{G}}}|\xi_{n}(g)||\gamma|(dg)\Bigr|^{p}\leq\sup_{\gamma\in C({\mathcal{G}})^{\ast},\|\gamma\|=1}\Bigl(\int_{{\mathcal{G}}}{\mathbb{E}}|\xi_{n}(g)|^{p}|\gamma|(dg)\Bigr)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{g\in{\mathcal{G}}}\|(P_{n}-P)(g)\|_{L_{p}({\mathbb{P}}_{P})}\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\vee U\frac{p}{n}.

∎

Lemma
7.1
implies the bound of Assumption
7.1
with
\sigma(P)=\sigma,U(P)=U.

It easily follows from bound (
7.4
) and Talagrand’s concentration inequality for empirical processes that, for all
p\geq 1,

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\|P_{n}-P\|_{{\mathcal{G}}}\Bigr\|_{L_{p}({\mathbb{P}}_{P})}\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{\frac{V}{n}}\vee U\frac{V}{n}\vee\sigma\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\vee U\frac{p}{n}.

(7.6)

Bound (
7.6
) implies the bound of Assumption
7.2
with
d_{1}(P)=\sigma^{2}V,d_{2}(P)=UV,\sigma(P)=\sigma,U(P)=U.

The claim now follows from the first bound of Theorem
7.1
.

∎

Other examples include estimation of functionals of sample covariance operators considered in Section
4

and functionals of parameters of high-dimensional exponential families discussed in Section
8
.

8
Exponential families: general background, Bernstein inequalities and further results

In this section, we provide a review of well known facts on exponential families (most of them, could be found in Chapter 2 of the book by Chencov
[
5
]
),
discuss Bernstein type bounds for estimators of the mean parameter and bounds on the
L_{p}
-errors of functional estimators.

8.1
Background on exponential families

Let
(S,{\mathcal{A}})
be a measurable space and let
\mu
be a
\sigma
-finite measure on
(S,{\mathcal{A}}).

Define probability measures
P_{\theta},\theta\in\Theta\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
on the space
(S,{\mathcal{A}})
to be absolutely continuous with respect to
\mu

with densities

\displaystyle p_{\theta}(x)=\frac{dP_{\theta}}{d\mu}(x)=\frac{1}{Z(\theta)}\exp\{\langle T(x),\theta\rangle\},x\in S,

(8.1)

where
T:S\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}^{N},

T(x)=(T_{1}(x),\dots,T_{N}(x)),x\in S
is a measurable function,
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is the canonical inner product in
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
and

\displaystyle Z(\theta):=\int_{S}\exp\{\langle T(x),\theta\rangle\}\mu(dx).

We assume that

\displaystyle\Theta:=\{\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}:Z(\theta)<+\infty\}\neq\emptyset.

It is well known and easy to check that
Z:{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\mapsto(0,+\infty]
is a convex function, implying that
\Theta
is a convex set.

The statistical model
\{P_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta\}
is called
the exponential family
generated by statistic
T
and measure
\mu.

Recall that
T(X),X\sim P_{\theta},\theta\in\Theta
is a sufficient statistic and it is complete when the interior of
\Theta
is nonempty:
{\rm Int}\Theta\neq\emptyset.

Clearly, the same exponential family could be generated by multiple choices of
T,\mu
and parameter
\theta.

For instance, one can obtain many different parametrizations of exponential family by applying invertible affine transformations
to parameter
\theta
or to statistic
T.

Note that,
for any fixed
\theta_{0}\in\Theta,
we have

\displaystyle\frac{dP_{\theta}}{dP_{\theta_{0}}}(x)=\frac{Z(\theta_{0})}{Z(\theta)}\exp\{\langle T(x),\theta-\theta_{0}\rangle\},

implying that
\{P_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta\}
can be also viewed as an exponential family generated by
T
and probability measure
P_{\theta_{0}}

with parameter
\theta-\theta_{0}\in\Theta-\theta_{0}.
Thus, without loss of generality, we can and will assume in what follows that
\mu
is a probability
measure, which implies that
0\in\Theta
and
Z(0)=1.

If
L:={\rm l.s.}(\Theta)
is a proper subspace of
{\mathbb{R}}^{N},
one can replace statistic
T
by its orthogonal projection onto
L.

Moreover, using the coordinate representation of
L
in an arbitrary orthonormal basis of
L,
subspace
L
can be identified with the Euclidean space

{\mathbb{R}}^{{\rm dim}(L)}
of dimension smaller than
N.
Thus, without loss of generality, we will assume in what follows that
{\rm l.s.}(\Theta)={\mathbb{R}}^{N}.

It is easy to see that such an exponential model is identifiable if the functions
1,T_{1},\dots,T_{N}
are linearly independent in the linear space of random variables
on
(S,{\mathcal{A}},\mu)
and such a linear independence can be always achieved by further reducing the number of functions
T_{1},\dots,T_{N}
and the dimension
of parameter
\theta.
So, again without loss of generality, we assume in what follows that functions
1,T_{1},\dots,T_{N}
are linearly independent, implying the identifiability
of the model
\{P_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta\}.

To summarize, we end up with the following assumption.

Assumption 8.1
.

Suppose the exponential family (
8.1
) satisfies:

(i)

\mu
is a probability measure on
(S,{\mathcal{A}});

(ii)

{\rm l.s.}(\Theta)={\mathbb{R}}^{N};

(iii)

functions
1,T_{1},\dots,T_{N}
are linearly independent in the linear space of random variables
on
(S,{\mathcal{A}},\mu).

Let

\displaystyle\psi(\theta):=\log Z(\theta)=\log\int_{S}\exp\{\langle T(x),\theta\rangle\}\mu(dx),\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}

be the cumulant generating function of r.v.
T(X),X\sim\mu.
The following properties of this function are
well known:

•

Function
\psi:{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}\cup{+\infty}
is convex and lower semi-continuous.

•

Moreover, under Assumption
8.1
(iii),
\psi
is strictly convex on
\Theta.

•

Z
and
\psi
are both analytic functions in the tube domain
\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}^{N}:{\rm Re}(z)\in{\rm Int}\Theta\}

and their derivatives could be calculated by differentiation under the integral sign.

•

In particular, the following formulas hold

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(\theta)=(\nabla\psi)(\theta)={\mathbb{E}}_{\theta}T(X),\theta\in{\rm Int}\Theta

and

\displaystyle\psi^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(\theta)={\mathbb{E}}_{\theta}(T(X)-{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta}T(X))\otimes(T(X)-{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta}T(x))={\rm Cov}_{\theta}(T(X)),\theta\in{\rm Int}\Theta.

•

Under Assumption
8.1
,
\psi^{\prime}
is strictly monotone on
{\rm Int}\Theta,
i.e.,

\displaystyle\langle\psi^{\prime}(\theta_{1})-\psi^{\prime}(\theta_{2}),\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\rangle>0,\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in{\rm Int}\Theta,\theta_{1}\neq\theta_{2},

and
\psi^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(\theta)
is positively definite for
\theta\in{\rm Int}\Theta.

Denote
\Psi(\theta):=\psi^{\prime}(\theta)={\mathbb{E}}_{\theta}T(X),\theta\in{\rm Int}\Theta.
It follows from the last property that
\Psi
is a bijection between

{\rm Int}\Theta
and
\Psi({\rm Int}\Theta),
which allows one to view
t=\Psi(\theta)={\mathbb{E}}_{\theta}T(X)
as an alternative parametrization of the sub-model
\{P_{\theta}:\theta\in{\rm Int}\Theta\}.
Note that parameter
t={\mathbb{E}}_{\theta}T(X)
is well defined for all
\theta\in\Theta_{1},
where

\displaystyle\Theta_{1}:=\{\theta\in\Theta:{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta}|T_{j}(X)|<\infty,j=1,\dots,N\}.

Clearly,
\Theta_{1}
is a convex set and
\Theta_{1}\supset{\rm Int}\Theta.

Thus, the mapping
\Psi
can be extended to the set
\Theta_{1}
and it could be proved that this extension (which will be still denoted by
\Psi
)
is a strictly monotone map on
\Theta_{1}
(see
[
5
]
, Chapter 4), implying that
\Psi
is a bijection between
\Theta_{1}
and its image

\Psi(\Theta_{1}).
Moreover,
\Psi
is infinitely differentiable (even analytic) in
{\rm Int}\Theta
with positive Jacobian at each point and
its inverse is also infinitely differentiable (
[
5
]
, Chapter 4). The parameter
t=\Psi(\theta)={\mathbb{E}}_{\theta}T(X)
of exponential
family is usually called
the mean parameter
, but we will rather call it
the natural parameter
(following the terminology of Chencov
[
5
]
).

Let
{\rm csupp}(\nu)
denote
the convex support
of a Borel probability measure
\nu
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
(that is, the smallest closed convex set of full measure
\nu,

or, in other words, the intersection of all closed half-spaces of full measure
\nu
). Since all measures
P_{\theta},\theta\in\Theta
are
equivalent to
\mu,
it is easy to see that
{\rm csupp}(P_{\theta}\circ T^{-1})={\rm csupp}(\mu\circ T^{-1}),\theta\in\Theta.
The following properties can be also found in
[
5
]
, Chapter 4:

1.

\Psi(\Theta_{1})\subset{\rm Int\ csupp}(\mu\circ T^{-1});

2.

\Psi(\Theta_{1})={\rm Int\ csupp}(\mu\circ T^{-1})
if and only if
\Theta_{1}={\rm Int}\Theta.

It will be said that the exponential model is
regular
iff
\Theta_{1}={\rm Int}\Theta

(or, equivalently,
\Psi(\Theta_{1})={\rm Int\ csupp}(\mu\circ T^{-1})
). For regular models,
the mapping
\Psi
is a
C^{\infty}
-diffeomorphism between open convex sets
\Theta_{1}={\rm Int}\Theta
and

{\rm Int\ csupp}(\mu\circ T^{-1}).

In this case, it makes sense to study the problem
of estimation of smooth functionals of natural parameter
t
in order to develop the methods of estimation of smooth
functionals of canonical parameter
\theta
(with the relationship between the two problems described in terms of diffeomorphism
\Psi
).
In particular, one can exploit the fact that there exists a simple unbiased estimator of natural parameter
t
based on i.i.d. observations

X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\sim P_{\Psi^{-1}(t)},
namely, the sample mean
\bar{T}_{n}:=\frac{T(X_{1})+\dots+T(X_{n})}{n}.

It is also known that, if
\bar{T}_{n}\in\Psi(\Theta_{1}),
then
\hat{\theta}_{n}:=\Psi^{-1}(\bar{T}_{n})
is the unique maximum likelihood
estimator of parameter
\theta\in\Theta_{1}.
Note also that the covariance
{\rm Cov}_{\theta}(T(X))=\Psi^{\prime}(\theta)
coincides with the Fisher
information matrix
I(\theta)
for the model
\{P_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta_{1}\}
whereas
{\mathcal{I}}(t):=I(\Psi^{-1}(t))^{-1}
is the Fisher
information for the model
P_{\Psi^{-1}(t)},t\in\Psi(\Theta_{1}).
This immediately implies that
\bar{T}_{n}
is an efficient estimator of natural parameter
t

and it provides a way to design asymptotically efficient estimators of
f(t).

It will be convenient for our purposes to assume now that statistic
T
takes values in an
N
-dimensional
linear normed space
E
with the dual space
E^{\ast}.
Recall that by choosing by-orthogonal bases
e_{1},\dots,e_{N}
and
f_{1},\dots,f_{N}

in
E
and
E^{\ast},
respectively, and using these bases to introduce coordinates in
E
and
E^{\ast},
we can identify these spaces
with the two copies of coordinate space
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
equipped with the corresponding norms.
With parameter
\theta\in E^{\ast},
densities
p_{\theta}
are still given by formula (
8.1
) and the parameter space

\Theta=\{\theta\in E^{\ast}:Z(\theta)<+\infty\}
is now a convex subset of
E^{\ast}.

We can still define
T_{j}(x):=\langle T(x),f_{j}\rangle,j=1,\dots,N.

Assumption
8.1
holds with
{\rm l.s.}(\Theta)=E^{\ast}
in part (ii). We still have the same properties of cumulant generating
function
\psi:E^{\ast}\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}\cup{+\infty}
with
\psi^{\prime}(\theta)
now taking values in space
E
and
\psi^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(\theta)
being a symmetric
linear operator from
E^{\ast}
into
E
(the covariance operator of
T(X)
). Thus,
\Psi
is now a map from
\Theta\subset E^{\ast}
into
E,

the natural parameter
t
takes values in
E,

\Theta_{1}
is a subset of
E^{\ast}
and
{\rm Int\ csupp}(\mu\circ T^{-1})
is a subset of
E.

As before, the regularity means that
\Psi(\Theta_{1})={\rm Int\ csupp}(\mu\circ T^{-1}),
which holds if and only if
\Theta_{1}={\rm Int}\Theta.

Example 8.1
.

Consider an exponential model

\displaystyle P_{\theta}(dx)=\frac{1}{Z(\theta)}\exp\{\langle x,\theta\rangle\}\mu(dx),x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N},

where
\mu
is a Borel probability measure on
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
invariant with respect to the group of orthogonal transformations.
In this case, the function
Z(\theta)
is also orthogonally invariant and

Z(\theta)=\tilde{Z}(\|\theta\|),

where

\displaystyle\tilde{Z}(\rho):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{N}}e^{\rho x_{1}}\mu(dx),\rho\geq 0.

Let

\displaystyle r:=\sup\{\rho\geq 0:\tilde{Z}(\rho)<\infty\}.

Clearly, if
r=+\infty,
then
\Theta:=\{\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}:Z(\theta)<\infty\}={\mathbb{R}}^{N}
and, if
r=0,
then

\Theta=\{0\}.

Otherwise, for
r\in(0,+\infty),

\Theta
is either the open ball
B(0,r)
with center
0
and radius
r

(if
\tilde{Z}(r)=+\infty
), or closed ball
\bar{B}(0,r)
(if
\tilde{Z}(r)<+\infty
). We will assume that
r>0,
then

{\rm Int}\Theta=B(0,r)\neq\emptyset.

Clearly,

\psi(\theta)=\varphi(\|\theta\|),

where
\varphi(\rho)=\log\tilde{Z}(\rho),\rho\geq 0
is a strictly convex function on
[0,r).
Denote

\displaystyle\Phi(\rho):=\varphi^{\prime}(\rho),\rho\in[0,\rho),

which is a strictly increasing continuous function. Then we have

\displaystyle\Psi(\theta)=\Phi(\|\theta\|)\frac{\theta}{\|\theta\|},\theta\in B(0,r)

and

\displaystyle\Psi^{-1}(t)=\Phi^{-1}(\|t\|)\frac{t}{\|t\|},t\in B(0,\Phi(r)).

Note that both
\Psi
and
\Psi^{-1}
are spherically symmetric strictly monotone vector fields on the open balls
B(0,r)

and
B(0,\Phi(r)),
respectively. It is also easy to see that
\Phi(0)=0
(otherwise,
\Psi
could not be a one-to-one mapping).

Since measure
\mu
is orthogonally invariant, its convex support is a closed ball
\bar{B}(0,r^{\ast})
with center
0
and radius

r^{\ast}\in[0,\infty].
If
r^{\ast}<\infty,
then
r=+\infty
and
\Theta={\mathbb{R}}^{N}.

In this case,
\Theta_{1}=\Theta={\rm Int}\Theta={\mathbb{R}}^{N},
so the exponential model is regular and

\Psi({\mathbb{R}}^{N})=B(0,r^{\ast})={\rm Int}\ {\rm csupp}(\mu).

On the other hand, if
r^{\ast}=+\infty,
there are two possibilities. If
\tilde{Z}(r)=+\infty,
then
\Theta=B(0,r)={\rm Int}\Theta,

so again
\Theta_{1}={\rm Int}\Theta,
the model is regular
and
\Psi(B(0,r))=B(0,r^{\ast})={\mathbb{R}}^{N}.
If
\tilde{Z}(r)<\infty,
then
\Theta=\bar{B}(0,r)
and

\Theta_{1}
could be
either
B(0,r),
or
\bar{B}(0,r),
depending on divergence or convergence of the integral

\displaystyle\int_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N},x_{1}\geq 0}x_{1}e^{rx_{1}}\mu(dx).

If the integral diverges, then
\Theta_{1}=B(0,r)={\rm Int}\Theta,
the model is regular and
\Psi(B(0,r))={\mathbb{R}}^{N}.

Otherwise,
\Psi(B(0,r))=B(0,\Phi(r))
is a ball of finite radius in
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}.

Clearly, in all the regular cases,
r^{\ast}=\Phi(r),
otherwise
r^{\ast}>\Phi(r).

The smoothness properties of mappings
\Psi
and
\Psi^{-1}
clearly depend only on the smoothness of function

\Phi:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and its inverse
\Phi^{-1}.
Since the derivatives of
\Phi
can be represented in terms
of integrals
\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{N}}x_{1}^{k}e^{\rho x_{1}}\mu(dx),
the smoothness of
\Phi
and
\Phi^{-1}
is related to integrability
properties of measure
\mu.

Note that, when
\mu
is a uniform distribution on the unit sphere
S^{N-1},
the exponential family discussed in this example is called von Mises-Fisher distribution
and it provides an important statistical model for directional data.

8.2
Bernstein type inequalities for exponential families

We will consider an exponential family

\displaystyle P_{\theta}(dx)=\frac{1}{Z(\theta)}\exp\{\langle T(x),\theta\rangle\}\mu(dx),\theta\in\Theta\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{N}

under the assumptions and notations of Section
8.1
.
Denote

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\theta}:=\psi^{\prime\prime}(\theta)={\mathbb{E}}_{\theta}(T(X)-{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta}T(X))\otimes(T(X)-{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta}T(X)),\theta\in{\rm Int}\Theta.

For
\theta\in{\rm Int}\Theta
and
u\in E^{\ast},
let

\displaystyle\rho(\theta;u):=\sup\{r>0:\theta+ru\in{\rm Int}\Theta\}.

Clearly,
\rho(\theta,u)>0,\theta\in{\rm Int}\Theta,u\in E^{\ast}.

Also define

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}(\theta;u):=\sup_{\lambda\in(0,\rho(\theta;u))}\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{0}^{\lambda}\langle\Sigma_{\theta+su}u,u\rangle ds,\ \theta\in{\rm Int}\Theta,u\in E^{\ast}.

We will start with the following simple Bernstein type inequality (see
[
36
,
38
]
for other results on Bernstein type bounds for exponential models).

Proposition 8.1
.

For all
\theta\in{\rm Int}\Theta,u\in E^{\ast}
and
t>0
with probability at least
1-e^{-t},

\displaystyle\left|\langle\bar{T}_{n}-\mathbb{E}_{\theta}T(X),u\rangle\right|\leq\sqrt{2}\sigma(\theta;u)\sqrt{\frac{t}{n}}\bigvee\frac{2}{\rho(\theta;u)}\frac{t}{n}.

proof.

Recall that
\psi(\theta)=\log Z(\theta)
and
\psi^{\prime}(\theta)=\Psi(\theta)={\mathbb{E}}_{\theta}T(X).

This implies

\displaystyle\gamma(\lambda):=\log\mathbb{E}_{\theta}e^{\lambda\langle T(X)-\mathbb{E}_{\theta}T(X),u\rangle}

\displaystyle=\log\frac{Z(\theta+\lambda u)}{Z(\theta)}-\lambda\langle\mathbb{E}_{\theta}T(X),u\rangle

\displaystyle=\psi(\theta+\lambda u)-\psi(\theta)-\lambda\langle\psi^{\prime}(\theta),u\rangle.

Clearly,

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}(\lambda)=\langle\psi^{\prime}(\theta+\lambda u)-\psi^{\prime}(\theta),u\rangle

and

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)=\langle\psi^{\prime\prime}(\theta+\lambda u)u,u\rangle=\langle\Sigma_{\theta+\lambda u}u,u\rangle.

Since
\gamma(0)=0
and
\gamma^{\prime}(0)=0,
we get

\displaystyle\gamma(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\lambda}\int_{0}^{s}\gamma^{\prime\prime}(\tau)d\tau ds=\int_{0}^{\lambda}\int_{0}^{s}\langle\Sigma_{\theta+\tau u}u,u\rangle d\tau ds

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\lambda}\sigma^{2}(\theta;u)sds=\frac{\sigma^{2}(\theta;u)\lambda^{2}}{2},\lambda\in(0,\rho(\theta;u)).

The above inequality implies that, for
\lambda\in(0,n\rho(\theta;u)),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\exp\Bigl\{\lambda\langle\bar{T}_{n}-\mathbb{E}_{\theta}T(X),u\rangle\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta}\exp\Bigl\{\frac{\lambda}{n}\langle T(X_{j})-{\mathbb{E}}_{\theta}T(X),u\rangle\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=\exp\Bigl\{n\gamma\Bigl(\frac{\lambda}{n}\Bigr)\Bigr\}\leq\exp\Bigl\{\frac{\sigma^{2}(\theta;u)\lambda^{2}}{2n}\Bigr\},

implying that, for all
\delta>0,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\{\langle\bar{T}_{n}-\mathbb{E}_{\theta}T(X),u\rangle\geq\delta\}\leq\exp\Bigl\{\frac{\sigma^{2}(\theta;u)\lambda^{2}}{2n}-\lambda\delta\Bigr\}.

Minimizing the last bound over
\lambda\in(0,n\rho(\theta;u)),
we get

\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\{\langle\bar{T}_{n}-\mathbb{E}_{\theta}T(X),u\rangle\geq\delta\}\leq\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} &\exp\left\{-\frac{n\delta^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}(\theta;u)}\right\},\ \ \delta<\rho(\theta;u)\sigma^{2}(\theta;u)\\
&\exp\left\{-\frac{n\rho(\theta;u)\delta}{2}\right\},\ \ \delta\geq\rho(\theta;u)\sigma^{2}(\theta;u).\end{aligned}\end{cases}

It remains to take

\displaystyle\delta:=\sqrt{2}\sigma(\theta;u)\sqrt{\frac{t}{n}}\bigvee\frac{2}{\rho(\theta;u)}\frac{t}{n}

to complete the proof.

∎

One could easily see from the proof that the bound also holds with
\rho(\theta;u)
replaced by an arbitrary
0<\rho<\rho(\theta;u)
and
\sigma^{2}(\theta;u)
replaced by

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rho}^{2}(\theta;u):=\sup_{\lambda\in(0,\rho)}\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{0}^{\lambda}\langle\Sigma_{\theta+su}u,u\rangle ds,\ \theta\in{\rm Int}\Theta,u\in E^{\ast}.

If
B(\theta,\rho)=\{y\in E^{\ast}:\|y-\theta\|<\rho\}\subset{\rm Int}\Theta,
then
\rho(\theta;u)>\rho
for all
u\in E^{\ast},\|u\|\leq 1.

Also, for all such
u,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rho}^{2}(\theta;u)\leq\sup_{\theta^{\prime}\in B(\theta;\rho)}\|\Sigma_{\theta^{\prime}}\|=:\sigma_{\rho}^{2}(\theta).

Therefore, under the assumption that
B(\theta,\rho)\subset{\rm Int}\Theta,
for all
u\in E^{\ast},\|u\|\leq 1
and for all
t>0
